prose-gray dark:prose-invert [contain:inline-size] isolate empty:hidden mb-14" data-page-title="Securely deploying AI agents" data-page-href="/en/agent-sdk/secure-deployment" id="content"><span data-as="p">Claude Code and the Agent SDK can execute code, access files, and interact with external services on your behalf.</span>
<span data-as="p">Unlike traditional software that follows predetermined code paths, these tools generate their actions dynamically based on context and goals. This flexibility is what makes them useful, but it also means their behavior can be influenced by the content they process: files, webpages, or user input. This is sometimes called prompt injection. For example, if a repository’s README contains unusual instructions, Claude Code might incorporate those into its actions in ways the operator didn’t anticipate. This guide covers practical ways to reduce this risk.</span>
<span data-as="p">Not every deployment needs maximum security. A developer running Claude Code on their laptop has different requirements than a company processing customer data in a multi-tenant environment. This guide presents options ranging from Claude Code’s built-in security features to hardened production architectures, so you can choose what fits your situation.</span>
<h2 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="threat-model" aria-labelledby="_R_79deehkd74t9_"><a href="#threat-model" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Threat model"></a><span id="_R_79deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Threat model</span></h2>
<span data-as="p">Agents can take unintended actions due to prompt injection (instructions embedded in content they process) or model error. Claude models are designed to resist this; see the <a href="https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/models/overview" class="link" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer">model overview</a> and the system card for the model you deploy for evaluation details.</span>
<span data-as="p">Defense in depth is still good practice though. For example, if an agent processes a malicious file that instructs it to send customer data to an external server, network controls can block that request entirely.</span>
<h2 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="built-in-security-features" aria-labelledby="_R_d9deehkd74t9_"><a href="#built-in-security-features" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Built-in security features"></a><span id="_R_d9deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Built-in security features</span></h2>
<span data-as="p">Claude Code includes several security features that address common concerns. See the <a href="/docs/en/security" class="link">security documentation</a> for full details.</span>
<ul>
<li><strong>Permissions system</strong>: Permission rules decide whether a tool call runs, is blocked, or asks for approval first. For the forms a Bash rule misses and how rules combine with permission modes, see <a href="/docs/en/permissions#bash-rule-limits" class="link">What a Bash rule doesn’t match</a> and <a href="/docs/en/agent-sdk/permissions" class="link">Configure permissions</a>. Administrators can also set rules in <a href="/docs/en/permissions#managed-settings" class="link">managed settings</a>.</li>
<li><strong>Critical-path removals</strong>: With the permission mode set to <code>default</code>, an <code>rm</code> or <code>rmdir</code> command recognized as removing your working directory or another <a href="/docs/en/permission-modes#critical-paths" class="link">critical path</a> needs approval. A matching allow rule doesn’t skip that approval. This is a permission gate, not a sandbox. To control what the agent can write, use <a href="#filesystem-configuration" class="link">filesystem controls</a>.</li>
<li><strong>Web search summarization</strong>: Search results are summarized rather than passing raw content directly into the context, reducing the risk of prompt injection from malicious web content.</li>
<li><strong>Sandbox mode</strong>: Bash commands can run in a sandboxed environment that restricts filesystem and network access. See the <a href="/docs/en/sandboxing" class="link">sandboxing documentation</a> for details.</li>
</ul>
<h2 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="security-principles" aria-labelledby="_R_j9deehkd74t9_"><a href="#security-principles" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Security principles"></a><span id="_R_j9deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Security principles</span></h2>
<span data-as="p">For deployments that require additional hardening beyond Claude Code’s defaults, these principles guide the available options.</span>
<h3 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="security-boundaries" aria-labelledby="_R_n9deehkd74t9_"><a href="#security-boundaries" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Security boundaries"></a><span id="_R_n9deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Security boundaries</span></h3>
<span data-as="p">A security boundary separates components with different trust levels. For high-security deployments, you can place sensitive resources (like credentials) outside the boundary containing the agent. If something goes wrong in the agent’s environment, resources outside that boundary remain protected.</span>
<span data-as="p">For example, rather than giving an agent direct access to an API key, you could run a proxy outside the agent’s environment that injects the key into requests. The agent can make API calls, but it never sees the credential itself. This pattern is useful for multi-tenant deployments or when processing untrusted content.</span>
<h3 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="least-privilege" aria-labelledby="_R_t9deehkd74t9_"><a href="#least-privilege" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Least privilege"></a><span id="_R_t9deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Least privilege</span></h3>
<span data-as="p">When needed, you can restrict the agent to only the capabilities required for its specific task:</span>
<!-- --><div role="presentation" style="position:relative;--scroll-area-corner-height:0px;--scroll-area-corner-width:0px" data-component-part="scroll-area" data-table-wrapper="true" class="min-w-0 [--page-padding:20px] flex w-[calc(100%+(var(--page-padding)*2))] my-[1em] py-[1em] -mx-(--page-padding) max-w-none [contain:inline-size]"><div data-id="base-ui-_R_119deehkd74t9_-viewport" tabindex="-1" class="size-full rounded-[inherit] [--scroll-area-fade-size:32px] overflow-y-hidden! base-ui-disable-scrollbar" style="overflow:scroll" data-component-part="scroll-area-viewport" role="region" aria-label="Scrollable table"><div role="presentation" style="min-width:fit-content" data-component-part="scroll-area-content" class="flex"><div class="px-(--page-padding) grow max-w-none table"><table class="m-0 min-w-full w-full max-w-none table [&amp;_th]:text-left [&amp;_td[data-numeric]]:tabular-nums [&amp;_td]:min-w-[150px]"><thead><tr><th>Resource</th><th>Restriction options</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Filesystem</td><td>Mount only needed directories, prefer read-only</td></tr><tr><td>Network</td><td>Restrict to specific endpoints via proxy</td></tr><tr><td>Credentials</td><td>Inject via proxy rather than exposing directly</td></tr><tr><td>System capabilities</td><td>Drop Linux capabilities in containers</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div></div></div>
<h3 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="defense-in-depth" aria-labelledby="_R_139deehkd74t9_"><a href="#defense-in-depth" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Defense in depth"></a><span id="_R_139deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Defense in depth</span></h3>
<span data-as="p">For high-security environments, layering multiple controls provides additional protection. Options include:</span>
<ul>
<li>Container isolation</li>
<li>Network restrictions</li>
<li>Filesystem controls</li>
<li>Request validation at a proxy</li>
</ul>
<span data-as="p">The right combination depends on your threat model and operational requirements.</span>
<h2 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="isolation-technologies" aria-labelledby="_R_1b9deehkd74t9_"><a href="#isolation-technologies" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Isolation technologies"></a><span id="_R_1b9deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Isolation technologies</span></h2>
<span data-as="p">Different isolation technologies offer different tradeoffs between security strength, performance, and operational complexity.</span>
<div role="note" aria-label="Info" class="callout my-4 px-5 py-4 overflow-hidden rounded-2xl flex gap-3 border border-neutral-200 bg-neutral-50 dark:border-neutral-700 dark:bg-white/10" data-callout-type="info"><div class="mt-0.5 w-4" data-component-part="callout-icon"><svg aria-hidden="true" width="18" height="18" stroke-width="1.5" class="flex-none size-5 text-neutral-800 dark:text-neutral-300"><use href="#mi-mint-1-CalloutInfo"></use></svg></div><div class="text-sm prose dark:prose-invert min-w-0 w-full [&amp;_kbd]:bg-background-light dark:[&amp;_kbd]:bg-background-dark [&amp;_code]:text-current! [&amp;_kbd]:text-current! [&amp;_a]:text-current! [&amp;_a]:border-current [&amp;_strong]:text-current! text-neutral-800 dark:text-neutral-300" data-component-part="callout-content"><span data-as="p">In all of these configurations, Claude Code (or your Agent SDK application) runs inside the isolation boundary (the sandbox, container, or VM). The security controls described below restrict what the agent can access from within that boundary.</span></div></div>
<!-- --><div role="presentation" style="position:relative;--scroll-area-corner-height:0px;--scroll-area-corner-width:0px" data-component-part="scroll-area" data-table-wrapper="true" class="min-w-0 [--page-padding:20px] flex w-[calc(100%+(var(--page-padding)*2))] my-[1em] py-[1em] -mx-(--page-padding) max-w-none [contain:inline-size]"><div data-id="base-ui-_R_1h9deehkd74t9_-viewport" tabindex="-1" class="size-full rounded-[inherit] [--scroll-area-fade-size:32px] overflow-y-hidden! base-ui-disable-scrollbar" style="overflow:scroll" data-component-part="scroll-area-viewport" role="region" aria-label="Scrollable table"><div role="presentation" style="min-width:fit-content" data-component-part="scroll-area-content" class="flex"><div class="px-(--page-padding) grow max-w-none table"><table class="m-0 min-w-full w-full max-w-none table [&amp;_th]:text-left [&amp;_td[data-numeric]]:tabular-nums [&amp;_td]:min-w-[150px]"><thead><tr><th>Technology</th><th>Isolation strength</th><th>Performance overhead</th><th>Complexity</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Sandbox runtime</td><td>Good (secure defaults)</td><td>Very low</td><td>Low</td></tr><tr><td>Containers (Docker)</td><td>Setup dependent</td><td>Low</td><td>Medium</td></tr><tr><td>gVisor</td><td>Excellent (with correct setup)</td><td>Medium/High</td><td>Medium</td></tr><tr><td>VMs (Firecracker, QEMU)</td><td>Excellent (with correct setup)</td><td>High</td><td>Medium/High</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div></div></div>
<h3 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="sandbox-runtime" aria-labelledby="_R_1j9deehkd74t9_"><a href="#sandbox-runtime" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Sandbox runtime"></a><span id="_R_1j9deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Sandbox runtime</span></h3>
<span data-as="p">For lightweight isolation without containers, <a href="https://github.com/anthropics/sandbox-runtime" class="link" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer">sandbox-runtime</a> enforces filesystem and network restrictions at the OS level.</span>
<span data-as="p">The main advantage is simplicity: no Docker configuration, container images, or networking setup required. The proxy and filesystem restrictions are built in.</span>
<span data-as="p"><strong>How it works:</strong></span>
<ul>
<li><strong>Filesystem</strong>: Uses OS primitives (<code>bubblewrap</code> on Linux, <code>sandbox-exec</code> on macOS) to restrict read/write access to configured paths</li>
<li><strong>Network</strong>: Runs commands in their own network namespace (Linux) or under a Seatbelt profile (macOS) and routes their traffic through a built-in proxy that allows the configured domains. A connection to an outside host that skips the proxy fails.</li>
<li><strong>Configuration</strong>: JSON-based allowlists for domains and filesystem paths</li>
</ul>
<span data-as="p"><strong>Setup:</strong></span>
<span data-as="p">For the install, configuration, and launch steps, see <a href="/docs/en/sandbox-environments#set-up-and-launch-the-runtime" class="link">Set up and launch the runtime</a>.</span>
<span data-as="p"><strong>Security considerations:</strong></span>
<ol>
<li>
<span data-as="p"><strong>Same-host kernel</strong>: Unlike VMs, sandboxed processes share the host kernel. A kernel vulnerability could theoretically enable escape. For some threat models this is acceptable, but if you need kernel-level isolation, use gVisor or a separate VM.</span>
</li>
<li>
<span data-as="p"><strong>No TLS inspection</strong>: By default, the proxy enforces the allowlist based on the client-supplied hostname and doesn’t terminate or inspect encrypted traffic. Code running inside the sandbox can potentially use <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domain_fronting" class="link" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer">domain fronting</a> or similar techniques to reach hosts outside the allowlist. If your threat model requires stronger guarantees, use a TLS-terminating proxy that inspects traffic, as <a href="#traffic-forwarding" class="link">Traffic forwarding</a> describes. For more information, see the <a href="/docs/en/sandboxing#security-limitations" class="link">sandboxing security limitations</a>.</span>
</li>
<li>
<span data-as="p"><strong>Credentials for allowed domains</strong>: If the agent has permissive credentials for an allowed domain, ensure it cannot use that domain to trigger other network requests or to exfiltrate data.</span>
</li>
</ol>
<span data-as="p">For many single-developer and CI/CD use cases, sandbox-runtime raises the bar significantly with minimal setup. The sections below cover containers and VMs for deployments requiring stronger isolation.</span>
<h3 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="containers" aria-labelledby="_R_279deehkd74t9_"><a href="#containers" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Containers"></a><span id="_R_279deehkd74t9_" class="min-w-0 break-words cursor-pointer">Containers</span></h3>
<span data-as="p">Containers provide isolation through Linux namespaces. Each container has its own view of the filesystem, process tree, and network stack, while sharing the host kernel.</span>
<span data-as="p">A security-hardened container configuration might look like this:</span>
<div class="code-block mt-5 mb-8 not-prose rounded-2xl relative group min-w-0 print:print-color-exact text-gray-950 dark:text-gray-50 codeblock-light border border-gray-950/10 dark:border-white/10 <span data-as="p">Here’s what each option does:</span>
<!-- --><div role="presentation" style="position:relative;--scroll-area-corner-height:0px;--scroll-area-corner-width:0px" data-component-part="scroll-area" data-table-wrapper="true" class="min-w-0 [--page-padding:20px] flex w-[calc(100%+(var(--page-padding)*2))] my-[1em] py-[1em] -mx-(--page-padding) max-w-none [contain:inline-size]"><div data-id="base-ui-_R_2h9deehkd74t9_-viewport" tabindex="-1" class="size-full rounded-[inherit] [--scroll-area-fade-size:32px] overflow-y-hidden! base-ui-disable-scrollbar" style="overflow:scroll" data-component-part="scroll-area-viewport" role="region" aria-label="Scrollable table"><div role="presentation" style="min-width:fit-content" data-component-part="scroll-area-content" class="flex"><div class="px-(--page-padding) grow max-w-none table"><table class="m-0 min-w-full w-full max-w-none table [&amp;_th]:text-left [&amp;_td[data-numeric]]:tabular-nums [&amp;_td]:min-w-[150px]"><thead><tr><th>Option</th><th>Purpose</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td><code>--cap-drop ALL</code></td><td>Removes Linux capabilities like <code>NET_ADMIN</code> and <code>SYS_ADMIN</code> that could enable privilege escalation</td></tr><tr><td><code>--security-opt no-new-privileges</code></td><td>Prevents processes from gaining privileges through setuid binaries</td></tr><tr><td><code>--security-opt seccomp=...</code></td><td>Restricts available syscalls; Docker’s default blocks ~44, custom profiles can block more</td></tr><tr><td><code>--read-only</code></td><td>Makes the container’s root filesystem immutable, preventing the agent from persisting changes</td></tr><tr><td><code>--tmpfs /tmp:...</code></td><td>Provides a writable temporary directory that’s cleared when the container stops</td></tr><tr><td><code>--network none</code></td><td>Cuts the container off from external networks</td></tr><tr><td><code>--memory 2g</code></td><td>Limits memory usage to prevent resource exhaustion</td></tr><tr><td><code>--pids-limit 600</code></td><td>Limits processes and threads to prevent fork bombs</td></tr><tr><td><code>--user 1000:1000</code></td><td>Runs as a non-root user</td></tr><tr><td><code>-v ...:/workspace:ro</code></td><td>Mounts code read-only so the agent can analyze but not modify it. <strong>Avoid mounting sensitive host directories like <code>~/.ssh</code>, <code>~/.aws</code>, or <code>~/.config</code></strong></td></tr><tr><td><code>-v .../proxy.sock:...</code></td><td>Mounts a Unix socket connected to a proxy running outside the container (see below)</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div></div></div>
<span data-as="p"><strong>Unix socket architecture:</strong></span>
<span data-as="p">With <code>--network none</code>, a DNS lookup or a direct connection to another host fails inside the container. To let code in the container reach other hosts, run a proxy on the host and mount its Unix socket into the container. If you point <code>ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL</code> or <code>HTTP_PROXY</code> at a port on <code>localhost</code>, as <a href="#configuring-claude-code-to-use-a-proxy" class="link">Configuring Claude Code to use a proxy</a> shows, relay that port inside the container to the socket.</span>
<span data-as="p">You can set up this proxy to enforce domain allowlists, inject credentials, and log all traffic.</span>
<div role="note" aria-label="Warning" class="callout my-4 px-5 py-4 overflow-hidden rounded-2xl flex gap-3 border border-yellow-200 bg-yellow-50 dark:border-yellow-900 dark:bg-yellow-600/20 [&amp;_[data-component-part=&#x27;callout-icon&#x27;]]:mt-px" data-callout-type="warning"><div class="mt-0.5 w-4" data-component-part="callout-icon"><svg aria-hidden="true" width="18" height="18" stroke-width="1.5" class="flex-none size-5 text-yellow-800 dark:text-yellow-300"><use href="#mi-mint-1-CalloutWarning"></use></svg></div><div class="text-sm prose dark:prose-invert min-w-0 w-full [&amp;_kbd]:bg-background-light dark:[&amp;_kbd]:bg-background-dark [&amp;_code]:text-current! [&amp;_kbd]:text-current! [&amp;_a]:text-current! [&amp;_a]:border-current [&amp;_strong]:text-current! text-yellow-800 dark:text-yellow-300" data-component-part="callout-content"><span data-as="p">Within your proxy’s allowlist, the agent can send data to any allowed domain. Allow only the domains the agent needs.</span></div></div>
<span data-as="p"><strong>Additional hardening options:</strong></span>
<!-- --><div role="presentation" style="position:relative;--scroll-area-corner-height:0px;--scroll-area-corner-width:0px" data-component-part="scroll-area" data-table-wrapper="true" class="min-w-0 [--page-padding:20px] flex w-[calc(100%+(var(--page-padding)*2))] my-[1em] py-[1em] -mx-(--page-padding) max-w-none [contain:inline-size]"><div data-id="base-ui-_R_2t9deehkd74t9_-viewport" tabindex="-1" class="size-full rounded-[inherit] [--scroll-area-fade-size:32px] overflow-y-hidden! base-ui-disable-scrollbar" style="overflow:scroll" data-component-part="scroll-area-viewport" role="region" aria-label="Scrollable table"><div role="presentation" style="min-width:fit-content" data-component-part="scroll-area-content" class="flex"><div class="px-(--page-padding) grow max-w-none table"><table class="m-0 min-w-full w-full max-w-none table [&amp;_th]:text-left [&amp;_td[data-numeric]]:tabular-nums [&amp;_td]:min-w-[150px]"><thead><tr><th>Option</th><th>Purpose</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td><code>--userns-remap</code></td><td>Maps container root to unprivileged host user; requires daemon configuration but limits damage from container escape</td></tr><tr><td><code>--ipc private</code></td><td>Isolates inter-process communication to prevent cross-container attacks</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div></div></div>
<h3 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="gvisor" aria-labelledby="_R_2v9deehkd74t9_"><a href="#gvisor" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: gVisor"></a><span id="_R_2v9deehkd74t9_" class="min-w-0 break-words cursor-pointer">gVisor</span></h3>
<span data-as="p">Standard containers share the host kernel: when code inside a container makes a system call, it goes directly to the same kernel that runs the host. This means a kernel vulnerability could allow container escape. gVisor addresses this by intercepting system calls in userspace before they reach the host kernel, implementing its own compatibility layer that handles most syscalls without involving the real kernel.</span>
<span data-as="p">If an agent runs malicious code (perhaps due to prompt injection), that code runs in the container and could attempt kernel exploits. With gVisor, the attack surface is much smaller: the malicious code would need to exploit gVisor’s userspace implementation first and would have limited access to the real kernel.</span>
<span data-as="p">To use gVisor with Docker, install the <code>runsc</code> runtime and configure the daemon:</span>
<div class="code-block mt-5 mb-8 not-prose rounded-2xl relative group min-w-0 print:print-color-exact text-gray-950 bg-gray-50 dark:bg-white/5 dark:text-gray-50 codeblock-light border <span data-as="p"><strong>Performance considerations:</strong></span>
<!-- --><div role="presentation" style="position:relative;--scroll-area-corner-height:0px;--scroll-area-corner-width:0px" data-component-part="scroll-area" data-table-wrapper="true" class="min-w-0 [--page-padding:20px] flex w-[calc(100%+(var(--page-padding)*2))] my-[1em] py-[1em] -mx-(--page-padding) max-w-none [contain:inline-size]"><div data-id="base-ui-_R_3f9deehkd74t9_-viewport" tabindex="-1" class="size-full rounded-[inherit] [--scroll-area-fade-size:32px] overflow-y-hidden! base-ui-disable-scrollbar" style="overflow:scroll" data-component-part="scroll-area-viewport" role="region" aria-label="Scrollable table"><div role="presentation" style="min-width:fit-content" data-component-part="scroll-area-content" class="flex"><div class="px-(--page-padding) grow max-w-none table"><table class="m-0 min-w-full w-full max-w-none table [&amp;_th]:text-left [&amp;_td[data-numeric]]:tabular-nums [&amp;_td]:min-w-[150px]"><thead><tr><th>Workload</th><th>Overhead</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>CPU-bound computation</td><td>~0% (no syscall interception)</td></tr><tr><td>Simple syscalls</td><td>~2× slower</td></tr><tr><td>File I/O intensive</td><td>Up to 10-200× slower for heavy open/close patterns</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div></div></div>
<span data-as="p">For multi-tenant environments or when processing untrusted content, the additional isolation is often worth the overhead.</span>
<h3 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="virtual-machines" aria-labelledby="_R_3j9deehkd74t9_"><a href="#virtual-machines" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Virtual machines"></a><span id="_R_3j9deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Virtual machines</span></h3>
<span data-as="p">VMs provide hardware-level isolation through CPU virtualization extensions. Each VM runs its own kernel, creating a strong boundary. A vulnerability in the guest kernel doesn’t directly compromise the host. However, VMs aren’t automatically “more secure” than alternatives like gVisor. VM security depends heavily on the hypervisor and device emulation code.</span>
<span data-as="p">Firecracker is designed for lightweight microVM isolation. It can boot VMs in under 125ms with less than 5 MiB memory overhead, stripping away unnecessary device emulation to reduce attack surface.</span>
<span data-as="p">With this approach, the agent VM has no external network interface. Instead, it communicates through <code>vsock</code> (virtual sockets). All traffic routes through vsock to a proxy on the host, which enforces allowlists and injects credentials before forwarding requests.</span>
<h3 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="cloud-deployments" aria-labelledby="_R_3r9deehkd74t9_"><a href="#cloud-deployments" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Cloud deployments"></a><span id="_R_3r9deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Cloud deployments</span></h3>
<span data-as="p">For cloud deployments, you can combine any of the above isolation technologies with cloud-native network controls:</span>
<ol>
<li>Run agent containers in a private subnet with no internet gateway</li>
<li>Configure cloud firewall rules (AWS Security Groups, GCP VPC firewall) to block all egress except to your proxy</li>
<li>Run a proxy (such as <a href="https://www.envoyproxy.io/" class="link" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer">Envoy</a> with its <code>credential_injector</code> filter) that validates requests, enforces domain allowlists, injects credentials, and forwards to external APIs</li>
<li>Assign minimal IAM permissions to the agent’s service account, routing sensitive access through the proxy where possible</li>
<li>Log all traffic at the proxy for audit purposes</li>
</ol>
<h2 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="credential-management" aria-labelledby="_R_419deehkd74t9_"><a href="#credential-management" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Credential management"></a><span id="_R_419deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Credential management</span></h2>
<span data-as="p">Agents often need credentials to call APIs, access repositories, or interact with cloud services. The challenge is providing this access without exposing the credentials themselves.</span>
<h3 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="the-proxy-pattern" aria-labelledby="_R_459deehkd74t9_"><a href="#the-proxy-pattern" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: The proxy pattern"></a><span id="_R_459deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">The proxy pattern</span></h3>
<span data-as="p">The recommended approach is to run a proxy outside the agent’s security boundary that injects credentials into outgoing requests. The agent sends requests without credentials, the proxy adds them, and forwards the request to its destination.</span>
<span data-as="p">This pattern has several benefits:</span>
<ol>
<li>The agent never sees the actual credentials</li>
<li>The proxy can enforce an allowlist of permitted endpoints</li>
<li>The proxy can log all requests for auditing</li>
<li>Credentials are stored in one secure location rather than distributed to each agent</li>
</ol>
<h3 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="configuring-claude-code-to-use-a-proxy" aria-labelledby="_R_4d9deehkd74t9_"><a href="#configuring-claude-code-to-use-a-proxy" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Configuring Claude Code to use a proxy"></a><span id="_R_4d9deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Configuring Claude Code to use a proxy</span></h3>
<span data-as="p">Claude Code supports two methods for routing sampling requests through a proxy:</span>
<span data-as="p"><strong>Option 1: ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL (simple but only for sampling API requests)</strong></span>
<div class="code-block mt-5 mb-8 not-prose rounded-2xl relative group min-w-0 print:print-color-exact text-gray-950 dark:text-gray-50 codeblock-light border border-gray-950/10 dark:border-white/10 </code></pre></div></div></div></div><div class="code-block-fade-overlay print:hidden" data-fade-overlay="true" aria-hidden="true" style="--fade-color-light:#ffffff;--fade-color-dark:#0B0C0E"></div></div>
<span data-as="p">This tells Claude Code and the Agent SDK to send sampling requests to your proxy instead of the Claude API directly. Your proxy receives plaintext HTTP requests, can inspect and modify them (including injecting credentials), then forwards to the real API.</span>
<span data-as="p"><strong>Option 2: HTTP_PROXY / HTTPS_PROXY (system-wide)</strong></span>
<div class="code-block mt-5 mb-8 not-prose rounded-2xl relative group min-w-0 print:print-color-exact text-gray-950 dark:text-gray-50 codeblock-light border border-gray-950/10 dark:border-white/10 <span class="line"><span style="color:#CF222E;--shiki-dark:#569CD6">export</span><span style="color:#1F2328;--shiki-dark:#9CDCFE"> HTTPS_PROXY</span><span style="color:#CF222E;--shiki-dark:#D4D4D4">=</span><span style="color:#0A3069;--shiki-dark:#CE9178">&quot;http://localhost:8080&quot;</span></span>
</code></pre></div></div></div></div><div class="code-block-fade-overlay print:hidden" data-fade-overlay="true" aria-hidden="true" style="--fade-color-light:#ffffff;--fade-color-dark:#0B0C0E"></div></div>
<span data-as="p">Claude Code and the Agent SDK respect these standard environment variables, routing all HTTP traffic through the proxy. For HTTPS, the proxy creates an encrypted CONNECT tunnel: it cannot see or modify request contents without TLS interception.</span>
<h3 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="implementing-a-proxy" aria-labelledby="_R_4t9deehkd74t9_"><a href="#implementing-a-proxy" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Implementing a proxy"></a><span id="_R_4t9deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Implementing a proxy</span></h3>
<span data-as="p">You can build your own proxy or use an existing one:</span>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://www.envoyproxy.io/" class="link" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer">Envoy Proxy</a>: production-grade proxy with <code>credential_injector</code> filter for adding auth headers</li>
<li><a href="https://mitmproxy.org/" class="link" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer">mitmproxy</a>: TLS-terminating proxy for inspecting and modifying HTTPS traffic</li>
<li><a href="http://www.squid-cache.org/" class="link" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer">Squid</a>: caching proxy with access control lists</li>
<li><a href="https://github.com/BerriAI/litellm" class="link" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer">LiteLLM</a>: LLM gateway with credential injection and rate limiting</li>
</ul>
<h3 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="credentials-for-other-services" aria-labelledby="_R_539deehkd74t9_"><a href="#credentials-for-other-services" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Credentials for other services"></a><span id="_R_539deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Credentials for other services</span></h3>
<span data-as="p">Beyond sampling from the Claude API, agents often need authenticated access to other services, such as git repositories, databases, and internal APIs. There are two main approaches:</span>
<h4 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="custom-tools" aria-labelledby="_R_579deehkd74t9_"><a href="#custom-tools" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Custom tools"></a><span id="_R_579deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Custom tools</span></h4>
<span data-as="p">Provide access through an MCP server or custom tool that routes requests to a service running outside the agent’s security boundary. The agent calls the tool, but the actual authenticated request happens outside. The tool calls to a proxy which injects the credentials.</span>
<span data-as="p">For example, a git MCP server could accept commands from the agent but forward them to a git proxy running on the host, which adds authentication before contacting the remote repository. The agent never sees the credentials.</span>
<span data-as="p">Advantages:</span>
<ul>
<li><strong>No TLS interception</strong>: The external service makes authenticated requests directly</li>
<li><strong>Credentials stay outside</strong>: The agent only sees the tool interface, not the underlying credentials</li>
</ul>
<h4 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="traffic-forwarding" aria-labelledby="_R_5h9deehkd74t9_"><a href="#traffic-forwarding" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Traffic forwarding"></a><span id="_R_5h9deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Traffic forwarding</span></h4>
<span data-as="p">For Claude API calls, <code>ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL</code> lets you route requests to a proxy that can inspect and modify them in plaintext. But for other HTTPS services (GitHub, npm registries, internal APIs), the traffic is often encrypted end-to-end. Even if you route it through a proxy via <code>HTTP_PROXY</code>, the proxy only sees an opaque TLS tunnel and can’t inject credentials.</span>
<span data-as="p">To modify HTTPS traffic to arbitrary services, without using a custom tool, you need a TLS-terminating proxy that decrypts traffic, inspects or modifies it, then re-encrypts it before forwarding. This requires:</span>
<ol>
<li>Running the proxy outside the agent’s container</li>
<li>Installing the proxy’s CA certificate in the agent’s trust store (so the agent trusts the proxy’s certificates)</li>
<li>Configuring <code>HTTP_PROXY</code>/<code>HTTPS_PROXY</code> to route traffic through the proxy</li>
</ol>
<span data-as="p">This approach handles any HTTP-based service without writing custom tools, but adds complexity around certificate management.</span>
<span data-as="p">Note that not all programs respect <code>HTTP_PROXY</code>/<code>HTTPS_PROXY</code>. Most tools (curl, pip, npm, git) do, but some may bypass these variables and connect directly. For example, Node.js <code>fetch()</code> ignores these variables by default; in Node 24+ you can set <code>NODE_USE_ENV_PROXY=1</code> to enable support. To cover tools that ignore these variables, you can use <a href="https://github.com/haad/proxychains" class="link" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer">proxychains</a> to intercept network calls, or configure iptables to redirect outbound traffic to a transparent proxy.</span>
<div role="note" aria-label="Info" class="callout my-4 px-5 py-4 overflow-hidden rounded-2xl flex gap-3 border border-neutral-200 bg-neutral-50 dark:border-neutral-700 dark:bg-white/10" data-callout-type="info"><div class="mt-0.5 w-4" data-component-part="callout-icon"><svg aria-hidden="true" width="18" height="18" stroke-width="1.5" class="flex-none size-5 text-neutral-800 dark:text-neutral-300"><use href="#mi-mint-1-CalloutInfo"></use></svg></div><div class="text-sm prose dark:prose-invert min-w-0 w-full [&amp;_kbd]:bg-background-light dark:[&amp;_kbd]:bg-background-dark [&amp;_code]:text-current! [&amp;_kbd]:text-current! [&amp;_a]:text-current! [&amp;_a]:border-current [&amp;_strong]:text-current! text-neutral-800 dark:text-neutral-300" data-component-part="callout-content"><span data-as="p">A <strong>transparent proxy</strong> intercepts traffic at the network level, so the client doesn’t need to be configured to use it. Regular proxies require clients to explicitly connect and speak HTTP CONNECT or SOCKS. Transparent proxies (like Squid or mitmproxy in transparent mode) can handle raw redirected TCP connections.</span></div></div>
<span data-as="p">Both approaches still require the TLS-terminating proxy and trusted CA certificate. They just ensure traffic actually reaches the proxy.</span>
<h2 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="filesystem-configuration" aria-labelledby="_R_619deehkd74t9_"><a href="#filesystem-configuration" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Filesystem configuration"></a><span id="_R_619deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Filesystem configuration</span></h2>
<span data-as="p">Filesystem controls determine what files the agent can read and write.</span>
<h3 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="read-only-code-mounting" aria-labelledby="_R_659deehkd74t9_"><a href="#read-only-code-mounting" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Read-only code mounting"></a><span id="_R_659deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Read-only code mounting</span></h3>
<span data-as="p">When the agent needs to analyze code but not modify it, mount the directory read-only:</span>
<div class="code-block mt-5 mb-8 not-prose rounded-2xl relative group min-w-0 print:print-color-exact text-gray-950 dark:text-gray-50 codeblock-light border border-gray-950/10 dark:border-white/10 dark:twoslash-dark bg-transparent dark:bg-transparent" data-has-floating-buttons="" numberOfLines="1" language="shellscript"><div data-floating-buttons="true" class="absolute top-3 right-4 flex items-center gap-1.5 print:hidden"><div class="z-10 select-none" id="base-ui-_R_7le99deehkd74t9_" data-base-ui-tooltip-trigger=""><button class="size-6.5 flex items-center justify-center rounded-md group/code-snippet-feedback-button [.code-block_[data-floating-buttons]_&amp;]:bg-gray-100 dark:[.code-block_[data-floating-buttons]_&amp;]:bg-gray-800 [.code-block:has([data-fade-overlay])_[data-floating-buttons]_&amp;]:bg-transparent dark:[.code-block:has([data-fade-overlay])_[data-floating-buttons]_&amp;]:bg-transparent" id="code-snippet-feedback-button" aria-label="Report incorrect code"><svg aria-hidden="true" width="18" height="18" stroke-width="1.5" class="size-4 shrink-0 text-gray-400 Common files to exclude or sanitize before mounting:</span><div role="presentation" style="position:relative;--scroll-area-corner-height:0px;--scroll-area-corner-width:0px" data-component-part="scroll-area" data-table-wrapper="true" class="min-w-0 [--page-padding:20px] flex w-[calc(100%+(var(--page-padding)*2))] my-[1em] py-[1em] -mx-(--page-padding) max-w-none [contain:inline-size]"><div data-id="base-ui-_R_2mb9deehkd74t9_-viewport" tabindex="-1" class="size-full rounded-[inherit] [--scroll-area-fade-size:32px] overflow-y-hidden! base-ui-disable-scrollbar" style="overflow:scroll" data-component-part="scroll-area-viewport" role="region" aria-label="Scrollable table"><div role="presentation" style="min-width:fit-content" data-component-part="scroll-area-content" class="flex"><div class="px-(--page-padding) grow max-w-none table"><table class="m-0 min-w-full w-full max-w-none table [&amp;_th]:text-left [&amp;_td[data-numeric]]:tabular-nums [&amp;_td]:min-w-[150px]"><thead><tr><th>File</th><th>Risk</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td><code>.env</code>, <code>.env.local</code></td><td>API keys, database passwords, secrets</td></tr><tr><td><code>~/.git-credentials</code></td><td>Git passwords/tokens in plaintext</td></tr><tr><td><code>~/.aws/credentials</code></td><td>AWS access keys</td></tr><tr><td><code>~/.config/gcloud/application_default_credentials.json</code></td><td>Google Cloud ADC tokens</td></tr><tr><td><code>~/.azure/</code></td><td>Azure CLI credentials</td></tr><tr><td><code>~/.docker/config.json</code></td><td>Docker registry auth tokens</td></tr><tr><td><code>~/.kube/config</code></td><td>Kubernetes cluster credentials</td></tr><tr><td><code>.npmrc</code>, <code>.pypirc</code></td><td>Package registry tokens</td></tr><tr><td><code>*-service-account.json</code></td><td>GCP service account keys</td></tr><tr><td><code>*.pem</code>, <code>*.key</code></td><td>Private keys</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div></div></div><span data-as="p">Consider copying only the source files needed, or using <code>.dockerignore</code>-style filtering.</span></div></div>
<h3 class="flex whitespace-pre-wrap group font-semibold" id="writable-locations" aria-labelledby="_R_6d9deehkd74t9_"><a href="#writable-locations" class="anchor-link" aria-label="Navigate to header: Writable locations"></a><span id="_R_6d9deehkd74t9_" class="cursor-pointer">Writable locations</span></h3>
<span data-as="p">If the agent needs to write files, you have a few options depending on whether you want changes to persist:</span>
<span data-as="p">For ephemeral workspaces in containers, use <code>tmpfs</code> mounts that exist only in memory and are cleared when the container stops:</span>

<span data-as="p">If you want to review changes before persisting them, an overlay filesystem lets the agent write without modifying underlying files. Changes are stored in a separate layer you can inspect, apply, or discard. For fully persistent output, mount a dedicated volume but keep it separate from sensitive directories.</span>

This flexibility is what makes them useful, but it also means their behavior can be influenced by the content they process: files, webpages, or user input. This is sometimes called prompt injection. For example, if a repository’s README contains unusual instructions, Claude Code might incorporate those into its actions in ways the operator didn’t anticipate. This guide covers practical ways to reduce this risk.`}),`\n`,_jsx(_components.p,{children:`Not every deployment needs maximum security. The tool calls to a proxy which injects the credentials.`}),`\n`,_jsx(_components.p,{children:`For example, a git MCP server could accept commands from the agent but forward them to a git proxy running on the host, which adds authentication before contacting the remote repository. The agent never sees the credentials.`}),`\n`,_jsx(_components.p,{children:`Advantages:`}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.ul,{children:[`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`No TLS interception`}),`: The external service makes authenticated requests directly`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`Credentials stay outside`}),`: The agent only sees the tool interface, not the underlying credentials`]}),`\n`]}),`\n`,_jsx(Heading,{level:`4`,id:`traffic-forwarding`,children:`Traffic forwarding`}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.p,{children:[`For Claude API calls, `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`}),` lets you route requests to a proxy that can inspect and modify them in plaintext. But for other HTTPS services (GitHub, npm registries, internal APIs), the traffic is often encrypted end-to-end. Even if you route it through a proxy via `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`HTTP_PROXY`}),`, the proxy only sees an opaque TLS tunnel and can’t inject credentials.`]}),`\n`,_jsx(_components.p,{children:`To modify HTTPS traffic to arbitrary services, without using a custom tool, you need a TLS-terminating proxy that decrypts traffic, inspects or modifies it, then re-encrypts it before forwarding. This requires:`}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.ol,{children:[`\n`,_jsx(_components.li,{children:`Running the proxy outside the agent’s container`}),`\n`,_jsx(_components.li,{children:`Installing the proxy’s CA certificate in the agent’s trust store (so the agent trusts the proxy’s certificates)`}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[`Configuring `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`HTTP_PROXY`}),`/`,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`HTTPS_PROXY`}),` to route traffic through the proxy`]}),`\n`]}),`\n`,_jsx(_components.p,{children:`This approach handles any HTTP-based service without writing custom tools, but adds complexity around certificate management.`}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.p,{children:[`Note that not all programs respect `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`HTTP_PROXY`}),`/`,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`HTTPS_PROXY`}),`. Most tools (curl, pip, npm, git) do, but some may bypass these variables and connect directly. For example, Node.js `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`fetch()`}),` ignores these variables by default; in Node 24+ you can set `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`NODE_USE_ENV_PROXY=1`}),` to enable support. To cover tools that ignore these variables, you can use `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`https://github.com/haad/proxychains`,children:`proxychains`}),` to intercept network calls, or configure iptables to redirect outbound traffic to a transparent proxy.`]}),`\n`,_jsx(Info,{children:_jsxs(_components.p,{children:[`A `,_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`transparent proxy`}),` intercepts traffic at the network level, so the client doesn’t need to be configured to use it. Transparent proxies (like Squid or mitmproxy in transparent mode) can handle raw redirected TCP connections.`]})}),`\n`,_jsx(_components.p,{children:`Both approaches still require the TLS-terminating proxy and trusted CA certificate.
